Like… there are things I still do, and sometimes they feel less bad than just sitting there, if that makes sense. I might watch something, scroll on my phone, listen to music, maybe grab food I used to like, but it’s more like background noise or something to get through the evening.\n\nI keep trying to compare it to how it used to feel, and that’s where I get stuck. Because before, if I was into something, I didn’t have to analyze it. I’d just look forward to it. Now it’s more like, “Okay, this is fine, I guess.” Not terrible, not exactly enjoyable either. So I don’t know if that counts as pleasure. There might be small moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about how low I feel, but actual enjoyment… I don’t really notice much of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah, but I keep hesitating on the word “lost,” because it makes it sound like there was this clear moment where I stopped caring about things. It’s more like the interest drained out slowly, and now when I think about doing things I used to like, there’s this kind of blank feeling.\n\nLike, I can remember that I used to want to do certain things, or that they mattered to me, but that feeling doesn’t really show up the same way now. Sometimes I’ll still start something because I remember I used to enjoy it, but it’s almost like I’m doing an imitation of myself, if that makes sense. I’m going through the steps of being interested without actually feeling pulled toward it.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I have lost interest, or at least a lot of it. I just don’t always know how to describe it without making it sound more dramatic than it feels in the moment. It’s not dramatic. It’s just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “guilty” is a little hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around thinking, like, I committed some terrible wrong or anything that clear. It’s more that I second-guess myself a lot, and I can end up feeling like I’m probably the reason something didn’t go well, even when I don’t have a specific thing to point to.\n\nLike if a conversation feels awkward, or if I didn’t respond the right way, or if I think I disappointed someone, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder what I should’ve done differently. But I’m not always sure if that’s guilt exactly, or just being hard on myself. Sometimes it feels like blame, but in this vague way, like I’m just generally not doing enough or not handling things right.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess I do blame myself sometimes, maybe more than I should. But it’s not always tied to one big thing. It’s more like this background feeling that I’m falling short somehow, and then I look for reasons why it must be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know if I’ve thought about it in those exact terms. Like, I know there are times where I feel really worn down and I don’t want to keep dealing with everything, but when you say “harming myself” or “wished I wasn’t alive,” I kind of freeze up because I’m not sure where the line is between feeling overwhelmed and something more serious.\n\nI don’t think I’ve really sat with it clearly enough to give you a clean yes-or-no answer. It’s more like vague thoughts or feelings that are hard to name. If you mean, like, specific thoughts about doing something to myself, I’m not sure that’s how I’d describe it. But if you mean feeling like I don’t want to be stuck feeling this way, then… yeah, I guess that part is there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before you started feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yeah… energy is probably one of the bigger things I notice, honestly. It’s not just being tired after a long shift, because I know work can be tiring for anyone. It’s more like even when I technically should be rested — like if I have a day off, or I sleep in late — my body still feels worn out. Like I’m starting the day already behind.\n\nAt work, by the time I’m done rooming patients and trying to catch up on charts, it feels like I’ve used up everything. And I don’t mean in a normal “that was a busy day” way. It’s like there’s nothing left in the tank for anything else. I’ll think, okay, I should do laundry, or I could go shoot hoops for a little bit, or mess around with my electronics stuff, because I used to be able to do those things after work. But then I just feel this heaviness, like my body is saying no before I even start.\n\nAs far as moving slower… I don’t know if other people would notice it. Maybe I’m not, like, visibly dragging myself around all the time. But inside it feels slower. Everything takes more effort than it should, even small things. Like I’m pushing through mud or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’ve clearly noticed, at least. I mean, I don’t weigh myself that often, so it’s possible there’s been some small change and I just haven’t tracked it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.\n\nThere are days where I’ll wonder if I look a little different, but then I can’t tell if that’s real or just me staring at myself too long. So I guess the honest answer is no significant change that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly critical of yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I probably criticize myself sometimes, like anyone does, especially if I mess something up or feel like I should’ve handled something better. But “overly” is the part I’m not totally sure about.\n\nI don’t know if what goes through my head is harsher than normal, because I don’t really know what other people’s internal voice sounds like, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll replay things I said or did and think, why did I do that, or I should’ve done that better, but I don’t know if I’d call it constant or anything. It’s just… hard to measure from inside my own head.\n\nSo I guess I’d say maybe, at times, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s this clear thing I’ve noticed all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know. That word feels really strong to me. I don’t usually sit there and think, like, “I have no worth” in those exact words.\n\nI do have moments where I feel pretty low about myself, like I’m not doing enough or I’m not where I should be, especially compared to other people my age. But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling worthless. It’s more like… disappointed in myself, maybe, or like I’m not measuring up. And then I’ll kind of get stuck thinking about it for a while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I constantly feel like I have no value at all… I don’t think I’d say it that way. I’m just not totally sure how to separate “feeling bad about myself” from “worthless,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re asking, like hearing voices when no one’s there or seeing something that other people can’t see. I haven’t had anything like that.\n\nSometimes if I’m really tired or distracted I might think I heard my phone or someone saying something, and then it’s nothing, but I feel like that’s pretty normal? Like a noise in the hallway or something, not a voice talking to me. And I don’t see things that aren’t there. So… no, I don’t think that’s been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death recurring in your mind?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as “thoughts about death recurring.” Like, I don’t sit there planning anything or… I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nI think sometimes the idea of death crosses my mind in a more general way, like if I hear about someone dying or there’s something online, I’ll think about it for a bit. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly thinking about death over and over in a clear way.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say not really, or at least not that I’ve noticed as a repeated thing. It’s hard because some thoughts are just kind of there in the background, and then when you ask, I start second-guessing whether I’m underplaying it or overthinking the question. Sometimes I’ll still start something because I remember I used to enjoy it, but it’s almost like I’m doing an imitation of myself, if that makes sense. I’m going through the steps of being interested without actually feeling pulled toward it.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I have lost interest, or at least a lot of it. I just don’t always know how to describe it without making it sound more dramatic than it feels in the moment. It’s not dramatic. It’s just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “guilty” is a little hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around thinking, like, I committed some terrible wrong or anything that clear. It’s more that I second-guess myself a lot, and I can end up feeling like I’m probably the reason something didn’t go well, even when I don’t have a specific thing to point to.\n\nLike if a conversation feels awkward, or if I didn’t respond the right way, or if I think I disappointed someone, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder what I should’ve done differently. But I’m not always sure if that’s guilt exactly, or just being hard on myself. Sometimes it feels like blame, but in this vague way, like I’m just generally not doing enough or not handling things right.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess I do blame myself sometimes, maybe more than I should. But it’s not always tied to one big thing. It’s more like this background feeling that I’m falling short somehow, and then I look for reasons why it must be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know if I’ve thought about it in those exact terms. Like, I know there are times where I feel really worn down and I don’t want to keep dealing with everything, but when you say “harming myself” or “wished I wasn’t alive,” I kind of freeze up because I’m not sure where the line is between feeling overwhelmed and something more serious.\n\nI don’t think I’ve really sat with it clearly enough to give you a clean yes-or-no answer. It’s more like vague thoughts or feelings that are hard to name. If you mean, like, specific thoughts about doing something to myself, I’m not sure that’s how I’d describe it. But if you mean feeling like I don’t want to be stuck feeling this way, then… yeah, I guess that part is there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before you started feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yeah… energy is probably one of the bigger things I notice, honestly. It’s not just being tired after a long shift, because I know work can be tiring for anyone. It’s more like even when I technically should be rested — like if I have a day off, or I sleep in late — my body still feels worn out. Like I’m starting the day already behind.\n\nAt work, by the time I’m done rooming patients and trying to catch up on charts, it feels like I’ve used up everything. And I don’t mean in a normal “that was a busy day” way. It’s like there’s nothing left in the tank for anything else. I’ll think, okay, I should do laundry, or I could go shoot hoops for a little bit, or mess around with my electronics stuff, because I used to be able to do those things after work. But then I just feel this heaviness, like my body is saying no before I even start.\n\nAs far as moving slower… I don’t know if other people would notice it. Maybe I’m not, like, visibly dragging myself around all the time. But inside it feels slower. Everything takes more effort than it should, even small things. Like I’m pushing through mud or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’ve clearly noticed, at least. I mean, I don’t weigh myself that often, so it’s possible there’s been some small change and I just haven’t tracked it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.\n\nThere are days where I’ll wonder if I look a little different, but then I can’t tell if that’s real or just me staring at myself too long. So I guess the honest answer is no significant change that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly critical of yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I probably criticize myself sometimes, like anyone does, especially if I mess something up or feel like I should’ve handled something better. But “overly” is the part I’m not totally sure about.\n\nI don’t know if what goes through my head is harsher than normal, because I don’t really know what other people’s internal voice sounds like, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll replay things I said or did and think, why did I do that, or I should’ve done that better, but I don’t know if I’d call it constant or anything. It’s just… hard to measure from inside my own head.\n\nSo I guess I’d say maybe, at times, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s this clear thing I’ve noticed all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know. That word feels really strong to me. I don’t usually sit there and think, like, “I have no worth” in those exact words.\n\nI do have moments where I feel pretty low about myself, like I’m not doing enough or I’m not where I should be, especially compared to other people my age. But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling worthless. It’s more like… disappointed in myself, maybe, or like I’m not measuring up. And then I’ll kind of get stuck thinking about it for a while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I constantly feel like I have no value at all… I don’t think I’d say it that way. I’m just not totally sure how to separate “feeling bad about myself” from “worthless,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re asking, like hearing voices when no one’s there or seeing something that other people can’t see. I haven’t had anything like that.\n\nSometimes if I’m really tired or distracted I might think I heard my phone or someone saying something, and then it’s nothing, but I feel like that’s pretty normal? Like a noise in the hallway or something, not a voice talking to me. And I don’t see things that aren’t there. So… no, I don’t think that’s been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death recurring in your mind?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as “thoughts about death recurring.” Like, I don’t sit there planning anything or… I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nI think sometimes the idea of death crosses my mind in a more general way, like if I hear about someone dying or there’s something online, I’ll think about it for a bit. Or sometimes when I’m feeling really down, I might have this kind of heavy thought about life being exhausting, but I don’t know if that’s the same as death recurring in my mind. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly thinking about death over and over in a clear way.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say not really, or at least not that I’ve noticed as a repeated thing. It’s hard because some thoughts are just kind of there in the background, and then when you ask, I start second-guessing whether I’m underplaying it or overthinking the question. But no, I don’t think I’d describe it as recurring thoughts about death.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I feel really overwhelmed and tired of dealing with things, but I don’t think I’ve had thoughts like, “I’m going to end my life,” or making a plan or anything like that.\n\nIt’s hard to answer because when you ask it directly, I start scanning my memory and wondering if some vague thought counts. Like sometimes I’ll think, “I don’t want to feel like this,” or “I wish everything would just stop for a while,” but that feels different to me than actually wanting to kill myself. I don’t think I’ve crossed into thinking about ending my life in a real way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not really. At least not that I’d describe that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t say like, “I lost this many pounds.” I don’t think I’ve had some obvious major weight loss where my clothes are suddenly hanging off me or people are commenting on it.\n\nI mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little depending on how I’m eating or how busy I am, but I haven’t noticed a significant amount since I started feeling low. If it has changed, it’s not something I’ve clearly picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you feel significantly better than others, or is the low feeling pretty consistent?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty consistent, honestly. I mean, I do have moments where I’m distracted or I can get through a task at work and not think about it every second, but the overall feeling is kind of just… there.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. For a long time now I wake up already feeling low, before anything even happens that day. It’s like I open my eyes and there’s already this heavy gray feeling on me, like I’m carrying it before I even get to the clinic. And then it pretty much follows me through the whole day.\n\nSome days might be a little less intense than others, I guess, but I wouldn’t say I have days where I feel significantly better, like back to normal or anything. It’s more like the volume changes a little, but the low feeling is still playing in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where your low mood lifts and you feel almost back to normal, even if it's just for a short while?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might have little stretches where I’m busy enough that I’m not focusing on it as much, or I can kind of function and do what I need to do, but I wouldn’t say it actually lifts in a way where I feel back to normal.\n\nIt’s more like… the low feeling is there when I wake up. That’s actually a big reason I came in, because for a long time now I open my eyes and it’s already there, like this heavy gray feeling before I’ve even done anything or gotten to the clinic. And then it just kind of follows me through the day.\n\nSo even if there’s a short time where I’m distracted or maybe not as aware of it, underneath it still feels like it’s there. I don’t really get those clear breaks where I think, “Oh, I feel like myself again.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No… I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where it was gone or I felt significantly better.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I guess the simplest way to say it is I’ve been feeling really low for a long time. It’s not just like a bad morning or one rough week. I wake up and it’s already there, before anything even happens, like this heavy gray feeling sitting on me. And then I’ll get ready and go to the clinic, and it just kind of follows me through the whole day.\n\nI work as a medical assistant, so I’m around people and trying to stay professional, but inside it feels like I’m dragging myself through everything. It’s been going on long enough that I don’t really feel like I can just ignore it or wait for it to pass anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two years and… maybe two months now. I’ve thought about that a lot because it didn’t feel like it just suddenly appeared one day, but if I’m trying to pin it down, it really started around when the staffing changed at the clinic and my schedule started rotating all over the place.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just because I was tired or not used to the new hours, and that once things settled I’d feel normal again. I might watch something, scroll on my phone, listen to music, maybe grab food I used to like, but it’s more like background noise or something to get through the evening.\n\nI keep trying to compare it to how it used to feel, and that’s where I get stuck. Because before, if I was into something, I didn’t have to analyze it. I’d just look forward to it. Now it’s more like, “Okay, this is fine, I guess.” Not terrible, not exactly enjoyable either. So I don’t know if that counts as pleasure. There might be small moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about how low I feel, but actual enjoyment… I don’t really notice much of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah, but I keep hesitating on the word “lost,” because it makes it sound like there was this clear moment where I stopped caring about things. It’s more like the interest drained out slowly, and now when I think about doing things I used to like, there’s this kind of blank feeling.\n\nLike, I can remember that I used to want to do certain things, or that they mattered to me, but that feeling doesn’t really show up the same way now. Sometimes I’ll still start something because I remember I used to enjoy it, but it’s almost like I’m doing an imitation of myself, if that makes sense. But it’s not always tied to one big thing. It’s more like this background feeling that I’m falling short somehow, and then I look for reasons why it must be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know if I’ve thought about it in those exact terms. Like, I know there are times where I feel really worn down and I don’t want to keep dealing with everything, but when you say “harming myself” or “wished I wasn’t alive,” I kind of freeze up because I’m not sure where the line is between feeling overwhelmed and something more serious.\n\nI don’t think I’ve really sat with it clearly enough to give you a clean yes-or-no answer. It’s more like vague thoughts or feelings that are hard to name. If you mean, like, specific thoughts about doing something to myself, I’m not sure that’s how I’d describe it. But if you mean feeling like I don’t want to be stuck feeling this way, then… yeah, I guess that part is there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before you started feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yeah… energy is probably one of the bigger things I notice, honestly. It’s not just being tired after a long shift, because I know work can be tiring for anyone. It’s more like even when I technically should be rested — like if I have a day off, or I sleep in late — my body still feels worn out. Like I’m starting the day already behind.\n\nAt work, by the time I’m done rooming patients and trying to catch up on charts, it feels like I’ve used up everything. And I don’t mean in a normal “that was a busy day” way. It’s like there’s nothing left in the tank for anything else. I’ll think, okay, I should do laundry, or I could go shoot hoops for a little bit, or mess around with my electronics stuff, because I used to be able to do those things after work. But then I just feel this heaviness, like my body is saying no before I even start.\n\nAs far as moving slower… I don’t know if other people would notice it. Maybe I’m not, like, visibly dragging myself around all the time. But inside it feels slower. Everything takes more effort than it should, even small things. Like I’m pushing through mud or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’ve clearly noticed, at least. I mean, I don’t weigh myself that often, so it’s possible there’s been some small change and I just haven’t tracked it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.\n\nThere are days where I’ll wonder if I look a little different, but then I can’t tell if that’s real or just me staring at myself too long. So I guess the honest answer is no significant change that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly critical of yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I probably criticize myself sometimes, like anyone does, especially if I mess something up or feel like I should’ve handled something better. But “overly” is the part I’m not totally sure about.\n\nI don’t know if what goes through my head is harsher than normal, because I don’t really know what other people’s internal voice sounds like, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll replay things I said or did and think, why did I do that, or I should’ve done that better, but I don’t know if I’d call it constant or anything. It’s just… hard to measure from inside my own head.\n\nSo I guess I’d say maybe, at times, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s this clear thing I’ve noticed all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know. That word feels really strong to me. I don’t usually sit there and think, like, “I have no worth” in those exact words.\n\nI do have moments where I feel pretty low about myself, like I’m not doing enough or I’m not where I should be, especially compared to other people my age. But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling worthless. It’s more like… disappointed in myself, maybe, or like I’m not measuring up. And then I’ll kind of get stuck thinking about it for a while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I constantly feel like I have no value at all… I don’t think I’d say it that way. I’m just not totally sure how to separate “feeling bad about myself” from “worthless,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re asking, like hearing voices when no one’s there or seeing something that other people can’t see. I haven’t had anything like that.\n\nSometimes if I’m really tired or distracted I might think I heard my phone or someone saying something, and then it’s nothing, but I feel like that’s pretty normal? Like a noise in the hallway or something, not a voice talking to me. And I don’t see things that aren’t there. So… no, I don’t think that’s been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death recurring in your mind?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as “thoughts about death recurring.” Like, I don’t sit there planning anything or… I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nI think sometimes the idea of death crosses my mind in a more general way, like if I hear about someone dying or there’s something online, I’ll think about it for a bit. Or sometimes when I’m feeling really down, I might have this kind of heavy thought about life being exhausting, but I don’t know if that’s the same as death recurring in my mind. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly thinking about death over and over in a clear way.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say not really, or at least not that I’ve noticed as a repeated thing. It’s hard because some thoughts are just kind of there in the background, and then when you ask, I start second-guessing whether I’m underplaying it or overthinking the question. But no, I don’t think I’d describe it as recurring thoughts about death.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I feel really overwhelmed and tired of dealing with things, but I don’t think I’ve had thoughts like, “I’m going to end my life,” or making a plan or anything like that.\n\nIt’s hard to answer because when you ask it directly, I start scanning my memory and wondering if some vague thought counts. Like sometimes I’ll think, “I don’t want to feel like this,” or “I wish everything would just stop for a while,” but that feels different to me than actually wanting to kill myself. I don’t think I’ve crossed into thinking about ending my life in a real way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not really. At least not that I’d describe that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t say like, “I lost this many pounds.” I don’t think I’ve had some obvious major weight loss where my clothes are suddenly hanging off me or people are commenting on it.\n\nI mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little depending on how I’m eating or how busy I am, but I haven’t noticed a significant amount since I started feeling low. If it has changed, it’s not something I’ve clearly picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you feel significantly better than others, or is the low feeling pretty consistent?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty consistent, honestly. I mean, I do have moments where I’m distracted or I can get through a task at work and not think about it every second, but the overall feeling is kind of just… there.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. For a long time now I wake up already feeling low, before anything even happens that day. It’s like I open my eyes and there’s already this heavy gray feeling on me, like I’m carrying it before I even get to the clinic. And then it pretty much follows me through the whole day.\n\nSome days might be a little less intense than others, I guess, but I wouldn’t say I have days where I feel significantly better, like back to normal or anything. It’s more like the volume changes a little, but the low feeling is still playing in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where your low mood lifts and you feel almost back to normal, even if it's just for a short while?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might have little stretches where I’m busy enough that I’m not focusing on it as much, or I can kind of function and do what I need to do, but I wouldn’t say it actually lifts in a way where I feel back to normal.\n\nIt’s more like… the low feeling is there when I wake up. That’s actually a big reason I came in, because for a long time now I open my eyes and it’s already there, like this heavy gray feeling before I’ve even done anything or gotten to the clinic. And then it just kind of follows me through the day.\n\nSo even if there’s a short time where I’m distracted or maybe not as aware of it, underneath it still feels like it’s there. I don’t really get those clear breaks where I think, “Oh, I feel like myself again.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt significantly better and the low mood was gone?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where it was gone or I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s been going on for, I guess, about two years and two months now. I kind of trace it back to when the staffing at the clinic changed and my schedule started rotating all over the place. At first I thought it was just me adjusting or being worn down by the schedule, but it just never really let up.\n\nThere might be individual days where I’m more distracted or I’m getting through things better, but not a stretch like two months where I’d say the low mood was gone. It’s been pretty continuous since around then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s like I open my eyes and there’s already this heavy gray feeling on me, like I’m carrying it before I even get to the clinic. And then it pretty much follows me through the whole day.\n\nSome days might be a little less intense than others, I guess, but I wouldn’t say I have days where I feel significantly better, like back to normal or anything. It’s more like the volume changes a little, but the low feeling is still playing in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where your low mood lifts and you feel almost back to normal, even if it's just for a short while?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might have little stretches where I’m busy enough that I’m not focusing on it as much, or I can kind of function and do what I need to do, but I wouldn’t say it actually lifts in a way where I feel back to normal.\n\nIt’s more like… the low feeling is there when I wake up. That’s actually a big reason I came in, because for a long time now I open my eyes and it’s already there, like this heavy gray feeling before I’ve even done anything or gotten to the clinic. And then it just kind of follows me through the day.\n\nSo even if there’s a short time where I’m distracted or maybe not as aware of it, underneath it still feels like it’s there. I don’t really get those clear breaks where I think, “Oh, I feel like myself again.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt significantly better and the low mood was gone?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where it was gone or I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s been going on for, I guess, about two years and two months now. I kind of trace it back to when the staffing at the clinic changed and my schedule started rotating all over the place. At first I thought it was just me adjusting or being worn down by the schedule, but it just never really let up.\n\nThere might be individual days where I’m more distracted or I’m getting through things better, but not a stretch like two months where I’d say the low mood was gone. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I wish I could point to even a weekend or a few days where I felt like, okay, that’s me again, but I honestly can’t.

There are moments where I’m doing something and I’m not thinking about it as directly, like I’m moving through work or getting ready for the day, but it doesn’t feel like the low mood actually goes away. It’s more like it’s sitting underneath everything. I wake up and it’s already there, this heavy gray feeling, before I’ve even gotten to the clinic or had anything happen that day. And then it just follows me around.

So… no, I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt normal again. Maybe less aware of it for a little while, but not normal.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I guess the simplest way to say it is I’ve been feeling really low for a long time. It’s not just like a bad morning or one rough week. I wake up and it’s already there, before anything even happens, like this heavy gray feeling sitting on me. And then I’ll get ready and go to the clinic, and it just kind of follows me through the whole day.\n\nI work as a medical assistant, so I’m around people and trying to stay professional, but inside it feels like I’m dragging myself through everything. It’s been going on long enough that I don’t really feel like I can just ignore it or wait for it to pass anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two years and… maybe two months now. I’ve thought about that a lot because it didn’t feel like it just suddenly appeared one day, but if I’m trying to pin it down, it really started around when the staffing changed at the clinic and my schedule started rotating all over the place.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just because I was tired or not used to the new hours, and that once things settled I’d feel normal again. But it just kind of stayed, and then months turned into a year, and then another year. So, yeah… about two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I still do some of the same things I used to do, but I don’t always know if I’d call it “enjoying” them the way I used to. Sometimes I’ll put something on or go through the motions because it’s familiar, or because I feel like I should, but it’s hard for me to tell whether I’m actually getting anything out of it or just trying to fill time.\n\nI guess before, I didn’t really think about it — if I liked something, I just liked it. I might watch something, scroll on my phone, listen to music, maybe grab food I used to like, but it’s more like background noise or something to get through the evening.\n\nI keep trying to compare it to how it used to feel, and that’s where I get stuck. Because before, if I was into something, I didn’t have to analyze it. I’d just look forward to it. Now it’s more like, “Okay, this is fine, I guess.” Not terrible, not exactly enjoyable either. So I don’t know if that counts as pleasure. There might be small moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about how low I feel, but actual enjoyment… I don’t really notice much of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah, but I keep hesitating on the word “lost,” because it makes it sound like there was this clear moment where I stopped caring about things. It’s more like the interest drained out slowly, and now when I think about doing things I used to like, there’s this kind of blank feeling.\n\nLike, I can remember that I used to want to do certain things, or that they mattered to me, but that feeling doesn’t really show up the same way now. Sometimes I’ll still start something because I remember I used to enjoy it, but it’s almost like I’m doing an imitation of myself, if that makes sense. I’m going through the steps of being interested without actually feeling pulled toward it.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I have lost interest, or at least a lot of it. I just don’t always know how to describe it without making it sound more dramatic than it feels in the moment. It’s not dramatic. It’s just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “guilty” is a little hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around thinking, like, I committed some terrible wrong or anything that clear. It’s more that I second-guess myself a lot, and I can end up feeling like I’m probably the reason something didn’t go well, even when I don’t have a specific thing to point to.\n\nLike if a conversation feels awkward, or if I didn’t respond the right way, or if I think I disappointed someone, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder what I should’ve done differently. But I’m not always sure if that’s guilt exactly, or just being hard on myself. Sometimes it feels like blame, but in this vague way, like I’m just generally not doing enough or not handling things right.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess I do blame myself sometimes, maybe more than I should. But it’s not always tied to one big thing. It’s more like this background feeling that I’m falling short somehow, and then I look for reasons why it must be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know if I’ve thought about it in those exact terms. Like, I know there are times where I feel really worn down and I don’t want to keep dealing with everything, but when you say “harming myself” or “wished I wasn’t alive,” I kind of freeze up because I’m not sure where the line is between feeling overwhelmed and something more serious.\n\nI don’t think I’ve really sat with it clearly enough to give you a clean yes-or-no answer. It’s more like vague thoughts or feelings that are hard to name. If you mean, like, specific thoughts about doing something to myself, I’m not sure that’s how I’d describe it. But if you mean feeling like I don’t want to be stuck feeling this way, then… yeah, I guess that part is there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before you started feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yeah… energy is probably one of the bigger things I notice, honestly. It’s not just being tired after a long shift, because I know work can be tiring for anyone. It’s more like even when I technically should be rested — like if I have a day off, or I sleep in late — my body still feels worn out. Like I’m starting the day already behind.\n\nAt work, by the time I’m done rooming patients and trying to catch up on charts, it feels like I’ve used up everything. And I don’t mean in a normal “that was a busy day” way. It’s like there’s nothing left in the tank for anything else. I’ll think, okay, I should do laundry, or I could go shoot hoops for a little bit, or mess around with my electronics stuff, because I used to be able to do those things after work. But then I just feel this heaviness, like my body is saying no before I even start.\n\nAs far as moving slower… I don’t know if other people would notice it. Maybe I’m not, like, visibly dragging myself around all the time. But inside it feels slower. Everything takes more effort than it should, even small things. Like I’m pushing through mud or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’ve clearly noticed, at least. I mean, I don’t weigh myself that often, so it’s possible there’s been some small change and I just haven’t tracked it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.\n\nThere are days where I’ll wonder if I look a little different, but then I can’t tell if that’s real or just me staring at myself too long. So I guess the honest answer is no significant change that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly critical of yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I probably criticize myself sometimes, like anyone does, especially if I mess something up or feel like I should’ve handled something better. But “overly” is the part I’m not totally sure about.\n\nI don’t know if what goes through my head is harsher than normal, because I don’t really know what other people’s internal voice sounds like, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll replay things I said or did and think, why did I do that, or I should’ve done that better, but I don’t know if I’d call it constant or anything. It’s just… hard to measure from inside my own head.\n\nSo I guess I’d say maybe, at times, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s this clear thing I’ve noticed all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know. That word feels really strong to me. I don’t usually sit there and think, like, “I have no worth” in those exact words.\n\nI do have moments where I feel pretty low about myself, like I’m not doing enough or I’m not where I should be, especially compared to other people my age. But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling worthless. It’s more like… disappointed in myself, maybe, or like I’m not measuring up. And then I’ll kind of get stuck thinking about it for a while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I constantly feel like I have no value at all… I don’t think I’d say it that way. I’m just not totally sure how to separate “feeling bad about myself” from “worthless,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re asking, like hearing voices when no one’s there or seeing something that other people can’t see. I haven’t had anything like that.\n\nSometimes if I’m really tired or distracted I might think I heard my phone or someone saying something, and then it’s nothing, but I feel like that’s pretty normal? Like a noise in the hallway or something, not a voice talking to me. And I don’t see things that aren’t there. So… no, I don’t think that’s been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death recurring in your mind?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as “thoughts about death recurring.” Like, I don’t sit there planning anything or… I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nI think sometimes the idea of death crosses my mind in a more general way, like if I hear about someone dying or there’s something online, I’ll think about it for a bit. Or sometimes when I’m feeling really down, I might have this kind of heavy thought about life being exhausting, but I don’t know if that’s the same as death recurring in my mind. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly thinking about death over and over in a clear way.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say not really, or at least not that I’ve noticed as a repeated thing. It’s hard because some thoughts are just kind of there in the background, and then when you ask, I start second-guessing whether I’m underplaying it or overthinking the question. But no, I don’t think I’d describe it as recurring thoughts about death.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I feel really overwhelmed and tired of dealing with things, but I don’t think I’ve had thoughts like, “I’m going to end my life,” or making a plan or anything like that.\n\nIt’s hard to answer because when you ask it directly, I start scanning my memory and wondering if some vague thought counts. Like sometimes I’ll think, “I don’t want to feel like this,” or “I wish everything would just stop for a while,” but that feels different to me than actually wanting to kill myself. I don’t think I’ve crossed into thinking about ending my life in a real way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not really. At least not that I’d describe that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t say like, “I lost this many pounds.” I don’t think I’ve had some obvious major weight loss where my clothes are suddenly hanging off me or people are commenting on it.\n\nI mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little depending on how I’m eating or how busy I am, but I haven’t noticed a significant amount since I started feeling low. If it has changed, it’s not something I’ve clearly picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you feel significantly better than others, or is the low feeling pretty consistent?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty consistent, honestly. It’s just not clear to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s anything I’d confidently call pleasure right now. Like… there are things I still do, and sometimes they feel less bad than just sitting there, if that makes sense. I might watch something, scroll on my phone, listen to music, maybe grab food I used to like, but it’s more like background noise or something to get through the evening.\n\nI keep trying to compare it to how it used to feel, and that’s where I get stuck. Because before, if I was into something, I didn’t have to analyze it. I’d just look forward to it. Now it’s more like, “Okay, this is fine, I guess.” Not terrible, not exactly enjoyable either. So I don’t know if that counts as pleasure. There might be small moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about how low I feel, but actual enjoyment… I don’t really notice much of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah, but I keep hesitating on the word “lost,” because it makes it sound like there was this clear moment where I stopped caring about things. It’s more like the interest drained out slowly, and now when I think about doing things I used to like, there’s this kind of blank feeling.\n\nLike, I can remember that I used to want to do certain things, or that they mattered to me, but that feeling doesn’t really show up the same way now. Sometimes I’ll still start something because I remember I used to enjoy it, but it’s almost like I’m doing an imitation of myself, if that makes sense. I’m going through the steps of being interested without actually feeling pulled toward it.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I have lost interest, or at least a lot of it. I just don’t always know how to describe it without making it sound more dramatic than it feels in the moment. It’s not dramatic. It’s just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “guilty” is a little hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around thinking, like, I committed some terrible wrong or anything that clear. It’s more that I second-guess myself a lot, and I can end up feeling like I’m probably the reason something didn’t go well, even when I don’t have a specific thing to point to.\n\nLike if a conversation feels awkward, or if I didn’t respond the right way, or if I think I disappointed someone, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder what I should’ve done differently. But I’m not always sure if that’s guilt exactly, or just being hard on myself. Sometimes it feels like blame, but in this vague way, like I’m just generally not doing enough or not handling things right.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess I do blame myself sometimes, maybe more than I should. But it’s not always tied to one big thing. It’s more like this background feeling that I’m falling short somehow, and then I look for reasons why it must be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know if I’ve thought about it in those exact terms. Like, I know there are times where I feel really worn down and I don’t want to keep dealing with everything, but when you say “harming myself” or “wished I wasn’t alive,” I kind of freeze up because I’m not sure where the line is between feeling overwhelmed and something more serious.\n\nI don’t think I’ve really sat with it clearly enough to give you a clean yes-or-no answer. It’s more like vague thoughts or feelings that are hard to name. If you mean, like, specific thoughts about doing something to myself, I’m not sure that’s how I’d describe it. But if you mean feeling like I don’t want to be stuck feeling this way, then… yeah, I guess that part is there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before you started feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yeah… energy is probably one of the bigger things I notice, honestly. It’s not just being tired after a long shift, because I know work can be tiring for anyone. It’s more like even when I technically should be rested — like if I have a day off, or I sleep in late — my body still feels worn out. Like I’m starting the day already behind.\n\nAt work, by the time I’m done rooming patients and trying to catch up on charts, it feels like I’ve used up everything. And I don’t mean in a normal “that was a busy day” way. It’s like there’s nothing left in the tank for anything else. I’ll think, okay, I should do laundry, or I could go shoot hoops for a little bit, or mess around with my electronics stuff, because I used to be able to do those things after work. But then I just feel this heaviness, like my body is saying no before I even start.\n\nAs far as moving slower… I don’t know if other people would notice it. Maybe I’m not, like, visibly dragging myself around all the time. But inside it feels slower. Everything takes more effort than it should, even small things. Like I’m pushing through mud or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’ve clearly noticed, at least. I mean, I don’t weigh myself that often, so it’s possible there’s been some small change and I just haven’t tracked it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.\n\nThere are days where I’ll wonder if I look a little different, but then I can’t tell if that’s real or just me staring at myself too long. So I guess the honest answer is no significant change that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly critical of yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I probably criticize myself sometimes, like anyone does, especially if I mess something up or feel like I should’ve handled something better. But “overly” is the part I’m not totally sure about.\n\nI don’t know if what goes through my head is harsher than normal, because I don’t really know what other people’s internal voice sounds like, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll replay things I said or did and think, why did I do that, or I should’ve done that better, but I don’t know if I’d call it constant or anything. It’s just… hard to measure from inside my own head.\n\nSo I guess I’d say maybe, at times, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s this clear thing I’ve noticed all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know. That word feels really strong to me. I don’t usually sit there and think, like, “I have no worth” in those exact words.\n\nI do have moments where I feel pretty low about myself, like I’m not doing enough or I’m not where I should be, especially compared to other people my age. But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling worthless. It’s more like… disappointed in myself, maybe, or like I’m not measuring up. And then I’ll kind of get stuck thinking about it for a while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I constantly feel like I have no value at all… I don’t think I’d say it that way. I’m just not totally sure how to separate “feeling bad about myself” from “worthless,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re asking, like hearing voices when no one’s there or seeing something that other people can’t see. I haven’t had anything like that.\n\nSometimes if I’m really tired or distracted I might think I heard my phone or someone saying something, and then it’s nothing, but I feel like that’s pretty normal? Like a noise in the hallway or something, not a voice talking to me. And I don’t see things that aren’t there. So… no, I don’t think that’s been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death recurring in your mind?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as “thoughts about death recurring.” Like, I don’t sit there planning anything or… I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nI think sometimes the idea of death crosses my mind in a more general way, like if I hear about someone dying or there’s something online, I’ll think about it for a bit. Or sometimes when I’m feeling really down, I might have this kind of heavy thought about life being exhausting, but I don’t know if that’s the same as death recurring in my mind. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly thinking about death over and over in a clear way.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say not really, or at least not that I’ve noticed as a repeated thing. It’s hard because some thoughts are just kind of there in the background, and then when you ask, I start second-guessing whether I’m underplaying it or overthinking the question. But no, I don’t think I’d describe it as recurring thoughts about death.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I feel really overwhelmed and tired of dealing with things, but I don’t think I’ve had thoughts like, “I’m going to end my life,” or making a plan or anything like that.\n\nIt’s hard to answer because when you ask it directly, I start scanning my memory and wondering if some vague thought counts. Like sometimes I’ll think, “I don’t want to feel like this,” or “I wish everything would just stop for a while,” but that feels different to me than actually wanting to kill myself. I don’t think I’ve crossed into thinking about ending my life in a real way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not really. At least not that I’d describe that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t say like, “I lost this many pounds.” I don’t think I’ve had some obvious major weight loss where my clothes are suddenly hanging off me or people are commenting on it.\n\nI mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little depending on how I’m eating or how busy I am, but I haven’t noticed a significant amount since I started feeling low. If it has changed, it’s not something I’ve clearly picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you feel significantly better than others, or is the low feeling pretty consistent?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty consistent, honestly. I mean, I do have moments where I’m distracted or I can get through a task at work and not think about it every second, but the overall feeling is kind of just… there.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. For a long time now I wake up already feeling low, before anything even happens that day. It’s like I open my eyes and there’s already this heavy gray feeling on me, like I’m carrying it before I even get to the clinic. And then it pretty much follows me through the whole day.\n\nSome days might be a little less intense than others, I guess, but I wouldn’t say I have days where I feel significantly better, like back to normal or anything. It’s more like the volume changes a little, but the low feeling is still playing in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where your low mood lifts and you feel almost back to normal, even if it's just for a short while?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. That’s actually a big reason I came in, because for a long time now I open my eyes and it’s already there, like this heavy gray feeling before I’ve even done anything or gotten to the clinic. And then it just kind of follows me through the day.\n\nSo even if there’s a short time where I’m distracted or maybe not as aware of it, underneath it still feels like it’s there. I don’t really get those clear breaks where I think, “Oh, I feel like myself again.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt significantly better and the low mood was gone?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where it was gone or I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s been going on for, I guess, about two years and two months now. I kind of trace it back to when the staffing at the clinic changed and my schedule started rotating all over the place. At first I thought it was just me adjusting or being worn down by the schedule, but it just never really let up.\n\nThere might be individual days where I’m more distracted or I’m getting through things better, but not a stretch like two months where I’d say the low mood was gone. It’s been pretty continuous since around then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period, no matter how short, in the past two years where you felt your normal self again?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wish I could point to even a weekend or a few days where I felt like, okay, that’s me again, but I honestly can’t.\n\nThere are moments where I’m doing something and I’m not thinking about it as directly, like I’m moving through work or getting ready for the day, but it doesn’t feel like the low mood actually goes away. It’s more like it’s sitting underneath everything. I wake up and it’s already there, this heavy gray feeling, before I’ve even gotten to the clinic or had anything happen that day. No… I really don’t think so. I mean, there are times where I’m more distracted or I’m just pushing through the day and maybe it’s not right in my face every second, but I wouldn’t call that significantly better.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Now I sort of have to check in with myself, like, “Am I enjoying this?” and even then I don’t know. It’s all kind of muted, maybe. But I don’t want to overstate it either, because there are moments where something is okay. It’s just not clear to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s anything I’d confidently call pleasure right now. Like… there are things I still do, and sometimes they feel less bad than just sitting there, if that makes sense. I might watch something, scroll on my phone, listen to music, maybe grab food I used to like, but it’s more like background noise or something to get through the evening.\n\nI keep trying to compare it to how it used to feel, and that’s where I get stuck. Because before, if I was into something, I didn’t have to analyze it. I’d just look forward to it. Now it’s more like, “Okay, this is fine, I guess.” Not terrible, not exactly enjoyable either. So I don’t know if that counts as pleasure. There might be small moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about how low I feel, but actual enjoyment… I don’t really notice much of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah, but I keep hesitating on the word “lost,” because it makes it sound like there was this clear moment where I stopped caring about things. It’s more like the interest drained out slowly, and now when I think about doing things I used to like, there’s this kind of blank feeling.\n\nLike, I can remember that I used to want to do certain things, or that they mattered to me, but that feeling doesn’t really show up the same way now. Sometimes I’ll still start something because I remember I used to enjoy it, but it’s almost like I’m doing an imitation of myself, if that makes sense. I’m going through the steps of being interested without actually feeling pulled toward it.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I have lost interest, or at least a lot of it. I just don’t always know how to describe it without making it sound more dramatic than it feels in the moment. It’s not dramatic. It’s just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “guilty” is a little hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around thinking, like, I committed some terrible wrong or anything that clear. It’s more that I second-guess myself a lot, and I can end up feeling like I’m probably the reason something didn’t go well, even when I don’t have a specific thing to point to.\n\nLike if a conversation feels awkward, or if I didn’t respond the right way, or if I think I disappointed someone, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder what I should’ve done differently. But I’m not always sure if that’s guilt exactly, or just being hard on myself. Sometimes it feels like blame, but in this vague way, like I’m just generally not doing enough or not handling things right.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess I do blame myself sometimes, maybe more than I should. But it’s not always tied to one big thing. It’s more like this background feeling that I’m falling short somehow, and then I look for reasons why it must be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know if I’ve thought about it in those exact terms. Like, I know there are times where I feel really worn down and I don’t want to keep dealing with everything, but when you say “harming myself” or “wished I wasn’t alive,” I kind of freeze up because I’m not sure where the line is between feeling overwhelmed and something more serious.\n\nI don’t think I’ve really sat with it clearly enough to give you a clean yes-or-no answer. It’s more like vague thoughts or feelings that are hard to name. If you mean, like, specific thoughts about doing something to myself, I’m not sure that’s how I’d describe it. But if you mean feeling like I don’t want to be stuck feeling this way, then… yeah, I guess that part is there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before you started feeling low?\n\nPatient: Yeah… energy is probably one of the bigger things I notice, honestly. It’s not just being tired after a long shift, because I know work can be tiring for anyone. It’s more like even when I technically should be rested — like if I have a day off, or I sleep in late — my body still feels worn out. Like I’m starting the day already behind.\n\nAt work, by the time I’m done rooming patients and trying to catch up on charts, it feels like I’ve used up everything. And I don’t mean in a normal “that was a busy day” way. It’s like there’s nothing left in the tank for anything else. I’ll think, okay, I should do laundry, or I could go shoot hoops for a little bit, or mess around with my electronics stuff, because I used to be able to do those things after work. But then I just feel this heaviness, like my body is saying no before I even start.\n\nAs far as moving slower… I don’t know if other people would notice it. Maybe I’m not, like, visibly dragging myself around all the time. But inside it feels slower. Everything takes more effort than it should, even small things. Like I’m pushing through mud or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’ve clearly noticed, at least. I mean, I don’t weigh myself that often, so it’s possible there’s been some small change and I just haven’t tracked it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.\n\nThere are days where I’ll wonder if I look a little different, but then I can’t tell if that’s real or just me staring at myself too long. So I guess the honest answer is no significant change that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly critical of yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I probably criticize myself sometimes, like anyone does, especially if I mess something up or feel like I should’ve handled something better. But “overly” is the part I’m not totally sure about.\n\nI don’t know if what goes through my head is harsher than normal, because I don’t really know what other people’s internal voice sounds like, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll replay things I said or did and think, why did I do that, or I should’ve done that better, but I don’t know if I’d call it constant or anything. It’s just… hard to measure from inside my own head.\n\nSo I guess I’d say maybe, at times, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s this clear thing I’ve noticed all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know. That word feels really strong to me. I don’t usually sit there and think, like, “I have no worth” in those exact words.\n\nI do have moments where I feel pretty low about myself, like I’m not doing enough or I’m not where I should be, especially compared to other people my age. But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling worthless. It’s more like… disappointed in myself, maybe, or like I’m not measuring up. And then I’ll kind of get stuck thinking about it for a while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I constantly feel like I have no value at all… I don’t think I’d say it that way. I’m just not totally sure how to separate “feeling bad about myself” from “worthless,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re asking, like hearing voices when no one’s there or seeing something that other people can’t see. I haven’t had anything like that.\n\nSometimes if I’m really tired or distracted I might think I heard my phone or someone saying something, and then it’s nothing, but I feel like that’s pretty normal? Like a noise in the hallway or something, not a voice talking to me. And I don’t see things that aren’t there. So… no, I don’t think that’s been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death recurring in your mind?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as “thoughts about death recurring.” Like, I don’t sit there planning anything or… I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nI think sometimes the idea of death crosses my mind in a more general way, like if I hear about someone dying or there’s something online, I’ll think about it for a bit. Or sometimes when I’m feeling really down, I might have this kind of heavy thought about life being exhausting, but I don’t know if that’s the same as death recurring in my mind. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly thinking about death over and over in a clear way.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say not really, or at least not that I’ve noticed as a repeated thing. It’s hard because some thoughts are just kind of there in the background, and then when you ask, I start second-guessing whether I’m underplaying it or overthinking the question. But no, I don’t think I’d describe it as recurring thoughts about death.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I feel really overwhelmed and tired of dealing with things, but I don’t think I’ve had thoughts like, “I’m going to end my life,” or making a plan or anything like that.\n\nIt’s hard to answer because when you ask it directly, I start scanning my memory and wondering if some vague thought counts. Like sometimes I’ll think, “I don’t want to feel like this,” or “I wish everything would just stop for a while,” but that feels different to me than actually wanting to kill myself. I don’t think I’ve crossed into thinking about ending my life in a real way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not really. At least not that I’d describe that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this low?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t say like, “I lost this many pounds.” I don’t think I’ve had some obvious major weight loss where my clothes are suddenly hanging off me or people are commenting on it.\n\nI mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little depending on how I’m eating or how busy I am, but I haven’t noticed a significant amount since I started feeling low. If it has changed, it’s not something I’ve clearly picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you feel significantly better than others, or is the low feeling pretty consistent?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty consistent, honestly. I mean, I do have moments where I’m distracted or I can get through a task at work and not think about it every second, but the overall feeling is kind of just… there.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. For a long time now I wake up already feeling low, before anything even happens that day. It’s like I open my eyes and there’s already this heavy gray feeling on me, like I’m carrying it before I even get to the clinic. And then it pretty much follows me through the whole day.\n\nSome days might be a little less intense than others, I guess, but I wouldn’t say I have days where I feel significantly better, like back to normal or anything. It’s more like the volume changes a little, but the low feeling is still playing in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where your low mood lifts and you feel almost back to normal, even if it's just for a short while?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might have little stretches where I’m busy enough that I’m not focusing on it as much, or I can kind of function and do what I need to do, but I wouldn’t say it actually lifts in a way where I feel back to normal.\n\nIt’s more like… the low feeling is there when I wake up. That’s actually a big reason I came in, because for a long time now I open my eyes and it’s already there, like this heavy gray feeling before I’ve even done anything or gotten to the clinic. And then it just kind of follows me through the day.\n\nSo even if there’s a short time where I’m distracted or maybe not as aware of it, underneath it still feels like it’s there. I don’t really get those clear breaks where I think, “Oh, I feel like myself again.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt significantly better and the low mood was gone?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where it was gone or I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s been going on for, I guess, about two years and two months now. I kind of trace it back to when the staffing at the clinic changed and my schedule started rotating all over the place. At first I thought it was just me adjusting or being worn down by the schedule, but it just never really let up.\n\nThere might be individual days where I’m more distracted or I’m getting through things better, but not a stretch like two months where I’d say the low mood was gone. It’s been pretty continuous since around then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period, no matter how short, in the past two years where you felt your normal self again?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wish I could point to even a weekend or a few days where I felt like, okay, that’s me again, but I honestly can’t.\n\nThere are moments where I’m doing something and I’m not thinking about it as directly, like I’m moving through work or getting ready for the day, but it doesn’t feel like the low mood actually goes away. It’s more like it’s sitting underneath everything. I wake up and it’s already there, this heavy gray feeling, before I’ve even gotten to the clinic or had anything happen that day. And then it just follows me around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt normal again. If it has changed, it’s not something I’ve clearly picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you feel significantly better than others, or is the low feeling pretty consistent?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty consistent, honestly. It’s more like the volume changes a little, but the low feeling is still playing in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where your low mood lifts and you feel almost back to normal, even if it's just for a short while?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might have little stretches where I’m busy enough that I’m not focusing on it as much, or I can kind of function and do what I need to do, but I wouldn’t say it actually lifts in a way where I feel back to normal.\n\nIt’s more like… the low feeling is there when I wake up. That’s actually a big reason I came in, because for a long time now I open my eyes and it’s already there, like this heavy gray feeling before I’ve even done anything or gotten to the clinic. And then it just kind of follows me through the day.\n\nSo even if there’s a short time where I’m distracted or maybe not as aware of it, underneath it still feels like it’s there. I don’t really get those clear breaks where I think, “Oh, I feel like myself again.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt significantly better and the low mood was gone?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where it was gone or I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s been going on for, I guess, about two years and two months now. I kind of trace it back to when the staffing at the clinic changed and my schedule started rotating all over the place. At first I thought it was just me adjusting or being worn down by the schedule, but it just never really let up.\n\nThere might be individual days where I’m more distracted or I’m getting through things better, but not a stretch like two months where I’d say the low mood was gone. It’s been pretty continuous since around then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period, no matter how short, in the past two years where you felt your normal self again?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wish I could point to even a weekend or a few days where I felt like, okay, that’s me again, but I honestly can’t.\n\nThere are moments where I’m doing something and I’m not thinking about it as directly, like I’m moving through work or getting ready for the day, but it doesn’t feel like the low mood actually goes away. It’s more like it’s sitting underneath everything. I wake up and it’s already there, this heavy gray feeling, before I’ve even gotten to the clinic or had anything happen that day. And then it just follows me around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt normal again. Maybe less aware of it for a little while, but not normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period, regardless of length, in the past two years where you felt your low mood significantly improved?\n\nPatient: No… I really don’t think so. I mean, there are times where I’m more distracted or I’m just pushing through the day and maybe it’s not right in my face every second, but I wouldn’t call that significantly better.\n\nThe reason I came in is because for a long time now it’s been there pretty much from the moment I wake up. And then it kind of just stays with me through the whole day.\n\nSo if you mean did it ever really lift in a noticeable way, even briefly… not really. It might fade into the background for a bit, but it doesn’t feel like it improves enough that I’d say I’m okay or back to normal."
